## Page 1


A CATEGORICAL p-ADIC LANGLANDS CORRESPONDENCE
FOR GL2(Qp)
ANDREA DOTTO, MATTHEW EMERTON, AND TOBY GEE
Abstract. Let p ≥5 be a prime. We construct a fully faithful functor from
the derived category of all smooth p-adic representations of GL2(Qp) (with
a fixed central character) to a derived category of Ind-coherent sheaves on a
stack of (φ, Γ)-modules.

1. Introduction
1.1. Overview of our results. We fix a prime p ≥5. Our goal in this paper is to
generalise the p-adic local Langlands correspondence for GL2(Qp). The existing form
of the correspondence (as initiated by Breuil, established in full generality by Colmez,
and then promoted to an equivalence of categories by Pašk¯unas) relates locally
admissible representations of GL2(Qp) to finitely generated modules over various
kinds of deformation rings for two-dimensional mod p representations of the absolute
Galois group Gal(Qp/Qp). Motivated by the “categorical” Langlands program,


## Page 3


A CATEGORICAL p-ADIC LANGLANDS CORRESPONDENCE FOR GL2(Qp)
3
our generalisation relates arbitrary (p-power torsion) smooth representations to
complexes of (Ind-) coherent sheaves on a moduli stack of (φ, Γ)-modules.
Our results were announced in the survey [EGH25], and we refer the reader to that
paper for an extensive introduction and motivation; here, we content ourselves with
emphasizing that the category of smooth representations is significantly larger than
that of locally admissible representations: for example, it contains the compactly
supported inductions of finite-dimensional representations of any open and compact
(modulo centre) subgroup of GL2(Qp), and these are never locally admissible.
To state our results more precisely, we introduce some notation. Let O denote
the ring of integers in a finite extension E of Qp, let F be the residue field of O, and
let A denote the category of smooth representations of G := GL2(Qp) on O-modules
on which p acts locally nilpotently, and which have a central character equal to some
fixed character ζ : Q×
p →O×. Let Db
fp(A) denote the full sub-∞-category of the
bounded derived category Db(A) of A consisting of objects with finitely presented
cohomology.
Let X be the formal algebraic stack over Spf O which parameterizes rank 2
projective étale (φ, Γ)-modules with fixed determinant ζε−1, where ε denotes the p-
adic cyclotomic character. Finally, let Db
coh(X) denote the bounded derived category
of Ind-coherent sheaves on X with coherent cohomologies. (We refer to Section B.2
for a precise definition of Db
coh(X).)
Our main theorem is then the following.
Theorem 1.1.1 (Definition 5.1.33 and Theorem 5.5.1). There exists an O-linear
fully faithful exact functor F : Db
fp(A) ,→Db
coh(X).
Using our assumption that p ≥5, we show that the unbounded derived category
is compactly generated by Db
fp(A), so for formal reasons, any functor F as in the
statement of Theorem 1.1.1 extends to a continuous (i.e. colimit-preserving) fully
faithful functor
F : D(A) →Ind Db
coh(X).
Thus Theorem 1.1.1 in fact gives a correspondence that takes account of all smooth
representations (and not just the finitely presented ones).
Remark 1.1.2. Although Theorem 1.1.1 is only phrased as an existence result,
the functor F that we construct in this paper enjoys several additional properties.
For example, Proposition 5.3.23 proves that F has amplitude [−1, 0], and Propo-
sition 5.3.20 describes some properties of the image under F of a natural set of
compact generators of D(A) (the compact inductions of Serre weights). These
results are in line with the general expectations described in [EGH25, Section 6].
The functor F is not an equivalence, and a sketch of the computation of its
essential image is given in [EGH25, Section 7.5.5]. We intend for the full details of
this computation to appear elsewhere.
1.1.3. Constructing the functor. We now describe our construction of the functor F.
We let OJGKζ denote the quotient of the completed group ring OJGK on which the
centre acts via ζ. Then general principles [EGH25, Rem. 6.1.23] suggest that F
must be of the form
(1.1.4)
F(π) = L∞⊗L
OJGKζ π,

A. DOTTO, M. EMERTON, AND T. GEE
for a pro-coherent sheaf L∞of right OJGKζ-modules on X. The first step in proving
our theorem is to directly construct a suitable pro-coherent sheaf L∞, and then use
the formula (1.1.4) to define the functor F.
Defined in this way, F a priori takes values in Pro Db
coh(X), and so one of our
problems is to show that F actually takes values in Db
coh(X). We then furthermore
have to show that F is fully faithful. Two key techniques used in the solution of these
problems are those of localization and completion, both in the geometric sense of
localizing or completing at points of X, and in a more categorical sense, in which we
pass from A to various of its quotient categories, or to blocks of its full subcategory
Al.adm of locally admissible representations.
1.1.5. Constructing the kernel. Our construction of L∞uses Colmez’s constructions
from [Col10c], extended to the context of (φ, Γ)-modules with coefficients in an
arbitrary Noetherian O/ϖa-algebra A. This extension is not formal, and it is the
subject of Section 4, which includes a systematic study of the base change properties
of Colmez’s functor D 7→D♮⊠P1.
We then construct L∞by descent in Section 5.1. To be precise, if Spf A →X is a
smooth chart, where A is an I-adically complete Noetherian O-algebra, then we may
form the corresponding pro-system of étale (φ, Γ)-modules DA/In. Via our extension
of Colmez’s constructions, we obtain a pro-system D♮
A/In ⊠P1 of OJGK-modules
on Spf A which admits descent data from Spf A to X. Descending it (and twisting
it appropriately), we obtain a pro-coherent sheaf of right OJGKζ-modules L∞on X.
1.1.6. Localization. The localization theory of [DEG23] can be briefly summarized
as follows. In [Paš13], Pašk¯unas shows that the blocks of Al.adm are in bijection
with Gal(F/F)-conjugacy classes of 2-dimensional continuous F-valued pseudorep-
resentations θ of GQp having determinant ζε−1, and we typically denote the block
of Al.adm corresponding to such a closed point by Aθ. In [DEG23] we explain that
these θ are parameterized by (the closed points of) a scheme X which is a chain of
projective lines over F, and we show that the category A localizes in a natural way
to a sheaf of abelian categories over X. Indeed, if Y is a closed subset of |X|, with
open complement U, then in [DEG23] we define a certain localizing subcategory
AY of A associated to Y , and set AU := A/AY . In the case when Y is finite, AY
consists, by construction, of locally admissible representations. In particular, when
Y = {θ} is a single closed point, then (again by construction) AY is the block Aθ.
By design, the process of passing to AU is related to localization of sheaves on X,
because the closed points of |X| are also in bijection with the (Galois conjugacy
classes of) residual pseudorepresentations θ, and the resulting bijection between
the closed points of |X| and |X| extends to a continuous morphism πss : |X| →|X|,
characterized by the following condition: for each pseudorepresentation θ, regarded
as a closed point of |X|, the completion Xθ of X along its closed subset π−1
ss (θ)
is the moduli stack of two-dimensional continuous Galois representations having
determinant ζε−1 whose underlying pseudorepresentations deform θ. (The stack Xθ
is an example of the moduli stacks of Galois representations constructed and studied
by Wang-Erickson in [Wan18].)
1.1.7. Establishing properties of F. As already remarked, having defined L∞, we
are able to define a functor
F := L∞⊗L
OJGKζ : Db
fp(A) →Pro Db
coh(X).

More precisely, part (1) of
the latter theorem is precisely the statement that F takes values in Db
coh(X);
while the full faithfulness of F follows by combining the full faithfulness results of
parts (3) and (4) of Theorem 1.1.8 with general category-theoretic arguments. These
arguments are developed in Appendix A.5; see especially Proposition A.5.3, which
provides a criterion for full faithfulness of a functor that preserves a recollement-like
decomposition of its source and target categories. Part (2) of Theorem 1.1.8 shows
that F falls within the scope of Proposition A.5.3, and requires the completion theory
of [DEG23] in its proof; in fact, we proceed by giving an independent definition of
the functor FY as a completion of F, and then proving that (1.1.9) commutes.
The proof of part (3) of Theorem 1.1.8 immediately reduces to the case in which
Y = {θ} is a singleton, in which case XY coincides with Xθ, and we write Fθ for FY .
As explained in the next paragraph, the full faithfulness of Fθ is a consequence
of the results of [JNW24]. This case of part (3) is then an input for the proof of
part (4), which also requires an explicit quotient presentation of the underlying
reduced Ured of the stack U, which we construct (for this specific choice of U) in
Section 3.7.
1.1.10. Relationship with other works. For a closed point θ ∈X, the functor Fθ
discussed above turns out to be very closely related to one of the functors constructed
by Johansson–Newton–Wang-Erickson in [JNW24]. Indeed, the only (relatively
minor) difference between our functor Fθ and the corresponding functor of [JNW24]
is that while our functor takes values in Db
coh(Xθ), where Xθ is the moduli stack
of Galois representations introduced above, theirs takes values in Db
coh(Xθ), for a
certain canonical algebraization Xθ of Xθ; and our functor is then obtained as a
completion of theirs. Given this fact, Theorem 1.1.8 (4) is essentially a restatement
of the full faithfulness results of [JNW24].


## Page 6


6
A. DOTTO, M. EMERTON, AND T. GEE
However, establishing this relationship between Fθ and the corresponding functor
of [JNW24] is non-trivial, since Fθ is defined in terms of Colmez’s D♮⊠P1 construc-
tion, while the functors of [JNW24] are defined by certain explicit kernels, using
Morita-theoretic descriptions of the various blocks Aθ due to Pašk¯unas [Paš13].
The relationship between the two is given by Proposition 5.2.18, which in partic-
ular provides a conceptual explanation for the particular choice of kernels made
in [JNW24].
More generally, we make full use of previously known results on the p-adic local
Langlands correspondence for GL2(Qp), including those of [Col10c; Paš13; PT21;
Paš15; JNW24; Kis09; HT15; San16]. We will often need to restate, or slightly
extend, these results to fit our framework, and we claim no real novelty in doing so.
1.1.11. Sheaf- and category-theoretic techniques. As this introduction already in-
dicates, we rely on Pro- and Ind-categorical techniques to deal with completions,
and to pass from the coherent/finitely presented setting to the setting of sheaves
and modules not satisfying any finiteness conditions. It seems likely that some, and
perhaps all, of the resulting technicalities could be ameliorated by using the solid
formalism of Clausen and Scholze; but for various reasons, including a lack of facility
in those techniques on our part, we have decided to adopt the more traditional
Ind/Pro perspective.
Our arguments also rely heavily on the theory of ∞-categories; in particular,
derived categories are, for us, always understood in the stable ∞-categorical sense.
The most substantial reason we require these techniques is that we prove our results
via gluing arguments, and the traditional framework of triangulated categories is
inadequate for making such arguments. We note, though, that we take advantage
of this formalism at many other points of our arguments as well; in particular, we
frequently exploit the flexibility it allows with regard to constructing and canonically
characterizing derived functors.
While these techniques (Ind/Pro-categories and ∞-categories) are standard tools
for experts in the categorical and geometric aspects of the Langlands program, they
may be less familiar to readers who (like ourselves) are approaching the paper from
the perspective of the traditional theory of the p-adic local Langlands correspondence.
To this end, and in order to have a uniform phrasing of several results from the
literature, we have included two appendices — Appendix A and Appendix B —
which provide background on category-theory (especially ∞-category theory) and
on coherent sheaf-theory on formal algebraic stacks.
1.1.12. A guide to the paper. Section 2 collects various background results from
Galois representation theory, smooth representation theory of GL2(Qp) in character-
istic p, and the existing theory of the local p-adic Langlands correspondence for this
group. We provide several complementary results, usually of a categorical nature,
to use later in the paper. We then describe the localization theory of [DEG23]
and generalize it to derived categories, and, using results of Heyer [Hey23; Hey24],
we prove some finiteness results for Ext-groups between certain non-admissible
representations.
Section 3 describes the Galois-theoretic side of our correspondence, building on
the results in Appendix C, and on several previous works in the literature, including
especially [EG23; Wan18; JNW24]. We then specialize the discussion of coherent,
Ind-coherent, and Pro-coherent sheaves of Appendix B to the present context of


## Page 7


A CATEGORICAL p-ADIC LANGLANDS CORRESPONDENCE FOR GL2(Qp)
7
moduli stacks of (φ, Γ)-modules, and carry out several explicit computations. We
conclude by building a connection between these objects and the localization theory
of [DEG23].
Section 4 contains our generalization of a significant part of Colmez’s work [Col10c],
to coefficients in arbitrary Noetherian O/ϖa-algebras. We begin by proving some
technical statements in Drinfeld’s theory of Tate modules, which is the natural
context for this generalization. We then work towards one of our main results,
which is Theorem 4.8.8 on the GL2(Qp)-stability of D♮⊠P1, and develop, along
the way, analogues of Colmez’s functors D+, D++, Dnr, D♯, D♮, and D ⊠−. We
make significant use of novel base change results, which will also be important in
Section 5 to construct L∞; these results can be specialized to the classical context
of coefficients in a complete Noetherian local O-algebra, and yield new results even
in that context (see Theorem 4.10.21).
Section 5 contains our construction of the functor F, and the proof of The-
orems 1.1.1 and 1.1.8. We begin by constructing a version of D♮⊠P1 for the
universal object on the stack X, which (as already explained) is done by descent,
as an application of the results of Section 4; the pro-coherent sheaf L∞is then
obtained as an appropriate twist of this universal version of D♮⊠P1. We next make
a detailed study of L∞, starting from its (completed) pullbacks to the stacks Xθ of
Galois representations, and of the functor F := L∞⊗OJGKζ −. Sections 5.3 and 5.4
establish the properties of F that are required to apply our full faithfulness criterion
(Proposition A.5.3), which we do in Section 5.5, where we conclude the proof of our
main result.
Appendix A assembles some background from 1-category and ∞-category theory,
with a significant focus on Ind- and Pro-completions of categories, which are the
context of our main full faithfulness criterion in Section A.5. One complication arises
from the interaction between Pro-completions and the formation of left derived
functors, which we study systematically in Sections A.8 and A.9.
As already
mentioned, much of this material is standard; but we have found it helpful to collect
it here, to give a uniform phrasing to several results from the literature, and to have
a uniform foundation for the less standard material in this appendix.
Appendix B develops the theory of coherent sheaves on formal algebraic stacks,
both at the abelian and derived level, including various functorial operations on
these objects, and recasting some of the coherent completeness results of [AHR23]
and [AHL23] in our framework. Finally, Appendix C provides some complements to
the results of [EG23] and [Wan18].
1.1.13. A comparison with the proof outlined in [EGH25]. The results of this paper
were announced in [EGH25, Section 7], which also gave a sketch of the proofs,
emphasizing the analogy between the results of [DEG23, Section 3.8], and Beauville–
Laszlo gluing of sheaves on X. This was presented as an important step in the
proof. Furthermore, we indicated a proof of part (4) of Theorem 1.1.8 via an explicit
Morita-theoretic argument.
However, in the course of writing this paper, we found a more efficient way of
formulating these gluing results, avoiding explicit Beauville–Laszlo-type gluing, and
proceeding instead via the notion of a recollement of ∞-categories. This resulted in
the current proof of the full faithfulness of F, as well as a simplification of the proof of
the Beauville–Laszlo-type results of [DEG23] (which has been incorporated into that
paper). Furthermore, we found a more efficient proof of part (4) of Theorem 1.1.8,

A. DOTTO, M. EMERTON, AND T. GEE
which avoids the explicit constructions and computations required in the Morita-
theoretic approach, and instead uses the same results related to completions that
are used to prove the full faithfulness of the functors FY for finite closed subsets Y
of X.
In conclusion, although the overall structure of the argument sketched in [EGH25]
has been preserved, and none of the statements of loc. cit. need to be corrected,
several of the details of our arguments are different from those sketched there.

1.3. Notation and conventions. We fix throughout the paper a prime p ≥5. Fix
an algebraic closure Qp of Qp. If K ⊂Qp is a finite extension of Qp, we write GK for
the absolute Galois group Gal(Qp/K). Write IK for the inertia subgroup of GK. We
normalise local class field theory so that a uniformizer corresponds to a geometric
Frobenius element.
Our convention for Hodge–Tate weights is that the p-adic
cyclotomic character ε : GQp →Q×
p has Hodge–Tate weight −1.
Let O denote the ring of integers in a fixed finite extension E of Qp, let ϖ be a
fixed uniformizer of O, and let F = O/ϖ be the residue field of O. Fix an algebraic
closure Fp of F. Unless otherwise stated, all representations considered in this
paper will be on O-modules. We fix a continuous character ζ : Q×
p →O×, which
we also regard as a character of GQp via local class field theory. Throughout the
paper we will work with representations of GL2(Qp) of central character ζ, and
representations of GQp with determinant ζε−1. We say that we are in the even
(resp. odd) case if ζ is an even (resp. odd) character.
We write G = GL2(Qp), K = GL2(Zp), and Z = Q×
p for the centre of G.
The diagonal torus in G is denoted by T, and we write B, resp. B for the upper-
triangular, resp. lower-triangular Borel subgroup containing T, with unipotent
radicals U and U. The maximal compact subgroup of T is denoted by T0. The
nth congruence subgroup of K is denoted Kn := 1 + pnM2(Zp), for n ≥1. The
upper-triangular Iwahori subgroup of K is denoted Iw, and we write Iw1 for its
pro-p Sylow subgroup and Z1 = Iw1 ∩Z = K1 ∩Z for the maximal pro-p subgroup
of Z. Since p ≥3, there are isomorphisms
(1.3.1)
Iw1
∼
−→Z1 × (Iw1/Z1) and K1
∼
−→Z1 × (K1/Z1),
given by the determinant and the projection to the quotient. Since p ≥5, the
groups Iw1 and K1 are torsion-free, and so the same is true for their direct fac-
tors Iw1/Z1 and K1/Z1.
If Z ⊂H ⊂G is a subgroup, we will write O[H]ζ for the quotient of the group
algebra O[H] by the two-sided ideal generated by {z −ζ(z) : z ∈Z}.
Stacks. Our conventions on algebraic stacks and formal algebraic stacks are those
of [Stacks] and [Eme]. The reader who is unfamiliar with this material may wish to
refer to the overview of [Eme] in [EG23, App. A]. If A is a topological ring of the


## Page 9


A CATEGORICAL p-ADIC LANGLANDS CORRESPONDENCE FOR GL2(Qp)
9
kind for which Spf A is defined (see [Stacks, Tag 0AIC] for an extensive discussion
of this; we won’t need any examples beyond I-adically complete Noetherian rings)
and C is a stack then we write C(A) for C(Spf A); if A has the discrete topology,
then this is equal to C(Spec A).
Module categories. We will consider several categories of modules over noncom-
mutative rings R. Unless stated otherwise, we will work with left modules. We
write Mod(R) for the category of left R-modules and R-linear maps, Modf.l.(R)
for the full subcategory of modules of finite length, and Modfp(R) for the full
subcategory of finitely presented modules, which coincides with the full subcategory
of compact objects of Mod(R). We will introduce several variants in the course of
the text, such as the category of profinite modules over a profinite topological ring,
see Section A.1.30. Recall that the Jacobson radical of R, denoted rad(R), is the
intersection of all maximal left ideals of R. It coincides with the intersection of all
maximal right ideals, respectively two-sided ideals, of R. Unless stated otherwise,
we write “Noetherian” as a shorthand for “left and right Noetherian”.
Category theory and homological algebra. We employ the language of ∞-categories
throughout the paper, and stable ∞-categories are the basic setting in which we
undertake our homological algebra. We also liberally employ the language of Ind and
Pro completion. Appendix A summarizes much of the material we use. Of course,
most of what we use can be found (often in more general form) in [Lur09], [Lur17],
and [Lur18]. On several occasions we cite [KS06], which provides an extensive
survey of many topics in homological algebra and category theory (in the traditional
language of derived categories). One caution we make is that its theory of Ind and
Pro categories is developed in the context of large categories, rather than in context
of small categories that we work with here.
We will be applying several of the constructions developed in [DEG23]. That
paper has a more abelian categorical flavour, and we wish to translate its results
into the derived context of this paper. The formation of left derived functors often
involves passing to Pro-categories, but for the applications we have in mind, we do
not wish to work in the derived category of the Pro-category, but rather in the Pro
category of the derived category. An explanation of how to arrange this is given in
§ A.8.
2. Background
In this section we gather together a range of results related to Galois represen-
tations as well as to smooth representations which we will require later on in the
paper.
2.1. Galois representations. We introduce various notation and concepts related
to two-dimensional representations and pseudorepresentations of GQp.

σa,p−2, of type (St) if it is of the form σa,0, σa+1,p−3, and otherwise we say
that it is of type (gen).

2.1.9. Pseudorepresentations. We will make use of the notion of a pseudorepresen-
tation of GQp on an O-algebra A. We will assume throughout, without further
comment, that all our pseudorepresentations are 2-dimensional and have deter-
minant ζε−1. Since p > 2, a 2-dimensional pseudorepresentation is equivalent to
the data of a 2-dimensional determinant in the sense of [Che14, Defn. 1.5], so
such a pseudorepresentation is equivalent (see [Che14, Lem. 1.9, Prop. 1.29]) to a
continuous function θ : GQp →A satisfying
• θ(1) = 2.
• θ(gh) = θ(hg).
• (ζε−1)(g)θ(g−1h) −θ(g)θ(h) + θ(gh) = 0.
Given a continuous representation ρ : GQp →GL2(A), the function tr ρ : GQp →
A is a pseudorepresentation in this sense. If A is a finite field, or an algebraically
closed field, then any pseudorepresentation comes from a representation, by [Wan18,
Corollary 2.9]. So we can and do identify F-valued pseudorepresentations with
semisimple representations with coefficients in F.
Definition 2.1.10. We classify the 2-dimensional Fp-valued pseudorepresentations
θ : GQp →Fp into five “types” as follows; we explain the labels in Remark 2.4.9.
(ssg) θ : GQp →Fp is the trace of an absolutely irreducible two-dimensional
F-representation.
(gen) θ = χ + ζω−1χ−1 for some character χ : GQp →F× such that the pair
{χ, ζω−1χ−1} is generic in the sense of Definition 2.1.2.
(scalar) θ = χ + χ for some character χ : GQp →F× such that χ2 = ζω−1. These
only exist if ζ is odd and ζ(p) is a square.
(St) θ = χ + χω−1 for some character χ : GQp →F× such that χ2 = ζ. These
only exist if ζ is even and ζ(p) is a square.
(gen+) θ = χ + ζω−1χ−1 for some character χ : GQp →F
×
p not defined over F.
Remark 2.1.11. The reason for the choice of terminology in Definition 2.1.8 is that
the semisimple Galois representations of type (scalar) (resp. of type (St)) have Serre
weights of the form σa,p−2 (respectively of the form σa,0, σa+1,p−3, σa,p−1).
Given any Fp-valued pseudorepresentation θ we write Fθ for the (finite) subfield
of Fp generated by F and the values of θ. It only depends on the Gal(Fp/F)-
conjugacy class of θ. We write (Rps
θ , θuniv) for the universal deformation of θ to
complete Noetherian local O ⊗W (F) W(Fθ)-algebras, where by a deformation of θ
to a complete local Noetherian O-algebra A with residue field Fθ, we mean a
2-dimensional pseudorepresentation θ : GQp →A, with determinant ζε−1, such that
the composition GQp
θ−→A →Fθ equals θ; the universal deformation exists by [Che14,
Prop. 3.3]. We sometimes refer to Rps
θ as the (universal) pseudodeformation ring
for θ.
Remark 2.1.12. We will sometimes say that a pseudorepresentation θ is F-rational
if Fθ = F. The first four items of Definition 2.1.10 are all F-rational, and clas-
sify pseudorepresentations attached to semisimple representations GQp →GL2(F)
all of whose irreducible summands are absolutely irreducible.

2.2.1. Abelian categories of representations. We say that an O-module is locally
torsion if each element is annihilated by some power of the uniformizer ϖ of O.
As usual, if Γ is a locally profinite group, then we say that a representation on a
locally torsion O-module is smooth if every element is fixed by an open subgroup
of Γ, and that a smooth representation is furthermore admissible if for any open
subgroup of Γ, its submodule of invariants is finite over O. Since we are working with
representations on p-power torsion modules, it suffices to check this for a single open
pro-p-subgroup of Γ (assuming that Γ admits such a subgroup). Following [Eme10,
Defn. 2.2.15], we say that a smooth representation π of Γ is locally admissible if
every vector v ∈π generates an admissible representation, and is locally finite if
every vector v ∈π generates a representation of finite length.
We write sm. Γ for the category of smooth Γ-representations on locally torsion
O-modules. Then sm. Γ is a Grothendieck category. (See e.g. the proof of [DEG23,
Lem. 2.2.3], which extends directly to any Γ.) We write (sm. Γ)adm, resp. (sm. Γ)l.adm,
(sm. Γ)f.l., (sm. Γ)fg, for the full subcategory of sm. Γ consisting of admissible objects,
resp. locally admissible objects, finite length objects, finitely generated objects.
We will be most interested in the case when Γ = G := GL2(Qp). We note that,
since the congruence subgroups Kn of G are open and pro-p, an object π of sm. G
is admissible if and only if πKn is O-finite for one (and hence every) n ≥1.
Throughout the paper we have fixed a continuous character ζ : Q×
p →O×,
and will focus on representations having fixed central character ζ. We therefore
introduce notation for the various categories of representations having this fixed
central character, which will be in force throughout the paper. Namely, we write A
for the full subcategory of sm. G consisting of objects with central character ζ, and
similarly Aadm, Al.adm, Af.l., Afg. Then Al.adm coincides with the category of locally
finite objects of A, by [Eme10, Thm. 2.2.17]. We have the following results about its
structure; recall (see Section A.1.25) that a locally finite category is a Grothendieck
category admitting a set of generators of finite length.

2.2.4. Completed group rings. If Γ◦is a profinite group, we write OJΓ◦K for the
usual Iwasawa algebra (i.e. completed group ring) of Γ◦with coefficients in O. It
is a profinite O-algebra. If Γ◦is a compact p-adic analytic group, then OJΓ◦K
is Noetherian [Laz65, Prop. V.2.2.4]. We also refer to [Eme10, Section 2.1] for a
recollection of some of the basic theory of these rings.
In fact, we will frequently consider Iwasawa coefficients with coefficients, especially
in the case when Γ◦= K. We recall the definition.
Definition 2.2.5. If A is an O-algebra, then AJΓ◦K denotes the topological A-
algebra
AJΓ◦K := lim
←−

where H runs over a neighbourhood basis of normal open subgroups of Γ◦, each
of the group rings A[Γ◦/H] is endowed with the discrete topology, and AJΓ◦K is
endowed with the projective limit topology.
We next record some properties of AJKK (under the additional hypothesis that
A is a finite type O/ϖa-algebra for some a ≥1). Before doing so, we recall from
e.g. [LO96, Definition II.2.1.1] that a filtered O-algebra (R, Fili R)i∈Z (possibly
non-commutative, decreasingly filtered, and with Fil0 = R) is called Zariskian if
Fil1 R ⊂rad(R) and the Rees ring

is Noetherian. We will use [LO96] as our main reference for this material, with the
caveat that we will work with decreasing filtrations, whereas loc. cit. uses increasing
filtrations. A filtered R-module M is good if Rees(M) is finitely generated over
Rees(R), which, by [LO96, Lem. I.5.4], is equivalent to the existence of generators
m1, . . . , mn of M as an R-module, and integers k1, . . . , kn, such that
Fili M = (Fili−k1 R)m1 + · · · + (Fili−kn R)mn.
Lemma 2.2.6. Let a be the kernel of OJK1K →F, i.e. the two-sided ideal generated
by ϖ and the augmentation ideal of OJK1K. Then the profinite topology on OJK1K
and OJKK is induced by the a-adic filtration, which is Zariskian.
Proof. First, recall that since K1 is a uniform pro-p group, the rings OJK1K and
gra(OJK1K) are Noetherian: since the a-adic filtration on OJK1K is induced by a
p-valuation on G, by [Ven02, Lem. 3.24, 3.25], this is a consequence of [Laz65,
Thm. III.2.3.3]. Since a = rad(OJK1K), we now see that the topology on OJK1K is
a-adic, by Lemma A.1.32 (8). Finally, since OJK1K is a-adically complete, [LO96,
Prop. II.2.2.2] shows that OJK1K is Zariskian, because the associated graded ring
graOJK1K is Noetherian. This concludes the proof for K1.

Since OJKK is a finite OJK1K-module, its a-adic filtration induces the profinite
topology, again by Lemma A.1.32 (8), and is a good filtration (by definition).
By [LO96, Thm. I.5.7], we conclude that gra(OJKK) is a finite gra(OJK1K)-module.
Hence gra(OJKK) is a Noetherian ring, which by another application of [LO96, Prop.
II.2.2.2] shows that OJKK is Zariskian with respect to the a-adic filtration.

Remark 2.2.10. As in the statement of Lemma 2.2.7, assume that A is of finite type
over O/ϖa for some a ≥1. Then since the ring A is then countable, the completed
group ring AJKK, endowed with its a-adic topology, is Polish. The general theory of
finitely generated modules over Noetherian Polish topological rings (recapitulated,
for example, in [EG23, Prop. C.6]) shows that any finitely generated AJKK-module
is endowed with a canonical completely metrizable topology, and that morphisms
between such modules are automatically continuous and strict with respect to this
topology.

Since the topology on AJKK is its a-adic topology, this canonical topology on
any finitely generated AJKK-module is again the a-adic topology. One may then
interpret Lemma 2.2.7 (5) as providing another proof of the strictness of morphisms
with respect to canonical topologies.
Remark 2.2.11. Suppose that A is a finite type O/ϖa-algebra for some a ≥1,
that M is an AJKK-module, and that M is finitely presented over A. Then M is
also finitely presented over AJKK, and its canonical topology is discrete. Indeed,
we can write the complete metric space M as a countable union of closed subsets
M = S
x∈M{x}, and if M were not discrete, each subset {x} would have empty
interior, contradicting the Baire category theorem.
Remark 2.2.12. Note that the forgetful functor induces an equivalence between
AJKK- Modfp(A) and the category OJKKa-tors- Modfp(A) of OJKK-module objects M
in Modfp(A) which are a-power torsion (in the sense that the OJKK-action factors
through OJKK/ai for some i, depending on the module). Indeed, the a-adic topology
on any M ∈AJKK- Modfp(A) is necessarily discrete, by Remark 2.2.11, hence M
is a-power torsion. The inverse functor endows each N ∈OJKKa-tors- Modfp(A)

we may replace AJKK- Modfp(A) with the category of a-power torsion OJKK-modules
in Modfp(A).

2.2.17. Smooth Γ-representations as OJΓK-modules in the non-compact case. If Γ is
a p-adic analytic group (compact or not), if Γ◦⊂Γ is a compact open subgroup,
and if π is an object of sm. Γ, then the Γ◦-action on π induces a unique continuous
OJΓ◦K-action on π (where we endow π with its discrete topology). This action can
be extended to a suitably completed group ring OJΓK of Γ. We recall its definition,
in the more general context where we allow coefficients.
Definition 2.2.18. If A is an O-algebra then we let
AJΓK := O[Γ] ⊗O[Γ◦] AJΓ◦K
with its natural ring structure, as explained in [Sho20, §3].
Lemma 2.2.19. If M is a Hausdorff topological A-module equipped with an action
of Γ by continuous automorphisms, and with a continuous AJΓ◦K-module structure,
such that the two resulting A[Γ◦]-module structures on M coincide, then the induced
A[Γ]-module structure on M extends uniquely to an AJΓK-module structure.
Proof. This is easily verified; see e.g. [Tim23, Prop. 2.23], whose proof carries over
verbatim.
□
As a special case of Lemma 2.2.19, we deduce the claim made above, namely that
if π ∈sm. Γ, then the action of OJΓ◦K on π induces an OJΓK-action on π. Thus we
obtain a fully faithful embedding sm. Γ ,→Mod(OJΓK). The following lemma shows
that this realizes sm. Γ as a localizing subcategory of Mod(OJΓK) (see Appendix A.1
for a brief recollection of this notion).
Lemma 2.2.20. The fully faithful embedding sm. Γ ,→Mod(OJΓK) realizes sm. Γ
as a localizing subcategory of Mod(OJΓK).
Remark 2.2.21. The main result of [Hey21] shows that sm. Γ is even a localizing
subcategory of Mod(O[Γ]), i.e. that extensions of smooth representations are auto-
matically smooth. However, we will not need this more general fact, but will satisfy
ourselves with recalling the (standard) proof of the easier result that we will be
using.
Proof of Lemma 2.2.20. It is immediate that the essential image of sm. Γ is closed
under subobjects, quotients, and colimits, and in particular under arbitrary direct
sums.

Lemma 2.2.23. The ring OJGKζ is coherent.
Proof. This is a consequence of [Tim23, Theorem 9.7] (see also [Sho20, Theorem 1.2]),
which states that OJGK is coherent, together with the fact that the quotient of a
coherent ring by an ideal that is generated by finitely many central elements is
coherent [Swa19, Corollary 3.6].

Lemma 2.2.31. The forgetful functor Ccoadm →Mod(OJGKζ) is fully faithful, and
its essential image is a Serre subcategory of its target.
Proof. The claimed full faithfulness is an immediate consequence of Lemma 2.2.30.

Noetherian profinite O-algebra OJKK, and (as noted above) is equipped with the
canonical compact topology it inherits by virtue of this. Thus, any OJGKζ-submodule
of M is again finitely generated over OJKK, and so is an object of Ccoadm. Similar
arguments in the case of quotients and extensions show that Ccoadm is indeed a
Serre subcategory of Mod(OJGKζ).
□

2.3. Morita theory for blocks of Al.adm and C. Unlike A, the category Al.adm
is locally finite, by Lemma 2.2.2. By the structure theory of locally finite categories,
recalled in Section A.1.25, Al.adm admits a decomposition into blocks. An extensive
analysis of the various blocks is made in [Paš13], and in what follows we recall,
and slightly expand, some of these results. The arguments in [Paš13] rely crucially
on Colmez’s functor “V ”, but we postpone our discussion of this functor until the
following section; in this section we focus on those results whose statements can be
made without reference to Colmez’s functor.

Because of Lemma 2.3.7, one can often assume without loss of generality that B
is F-rational. As already mentioned, these blocks have been thoroughly studied
in [Paš13], with the exception of F-rational blocks B of type (gen+); note that
these are not equivalent to blocks of type (gen), e.g. because they contain a unique
irreducible object. However, many of the invariants of B that we define in the
following sections become isomorphic to invariants of blocks of type (gen) after a
base extension, which will be enough for our intended applications.
2.3.9. The dual category associated to a block. We write CB for the image of AB
under Pontrjagin duality. By Proposition A.1.28, there exists a pseudocompact
O-algebra EB such that CB is equivalent to the category of pseudocompact right
EB-modules. The ring EB is only well-defined up to its category of pseudocompact
modules, and one of the main results of Pašk¯unas in [Paš13] is the computation of
an explicit choice of EB, and of its centre. We will recall these results in detail in
the next section. For now, we content ourselves by noting the following particular
lemma, which follows directly from those more precise results, and then deriving
some consequences of it. We do this because [Paš13] only considers blocks that
contain absolutely irreducible representations (i.e. not of type (gen+)) whereas it
will be important for us to have a uniform statement for all blocks. This will recur
throughout our discussion of “classical” p-adic local Langlands for GL2(Qp) (for
example, the absolutely irreducible case of Lemma 2.3.11 (3) can be found in [PT21,
Corollary 6.7]), and as we already intimated in Section 1.1.10, these amplifications
of the existing literature are straightforward consequences of those existing results.

B , M) for
some n ≥0, which is a quotient of HomCB(P ⊕n

Lemma 2.3.11. Let P be a projective object of CB with cosocle of finite length.
(1) The endomorphism ring E := EndCB(P) is a Noetherian profinite O-algebra,
and P is a Noetherian object of C.
(2) The module P, with its natural topology as an object of CB, is an object
of Modc(E).
(3) For all i > 0, the quotient P/ rad(E)iP is a finite length, hence coadmissible,
object of CB.
(4) If P is furthermore a projective generator of CB, then P is topologically flat
over E (hence projective in Modc(E), by Lemma A.1.44) and E has finite
global dimension.
Proof. Proof of (1): An application of Lemma 2.3.10 shows that E is a (left and right)
Noetherian profinite O-algebra. To see that P is Noetherian in C, or equivalently
in CB, note that there exists a projective generator PB of CB, with finite cosocle
and Noetherian endomorphism ring EB, such that P is a quotient of PB. Under the
equivalence HomCB(PB, –) : CB →Modc(Eop
B ), P goes to a quotient of EB, which
is a Noetherian Eop
B -module. Hence P is Noetherian in CB.
Proof of (2): Since the natural topology on P is profinite, it suffices to prove that
every open O-submodule of P of finite index in P contains an open left E-submodule,
which is exactly what is proved in [Paš13, Lemma 2.7]. (Strictly speaking, this
reference works under the additional assumption that cosoc(P) is multiplicity free,
but this assumption is only used to ensure that E/ rad(E) has finite F-dimension,
which follows from the fact that E is compact and has the rad(E)-adic topology, by
Lemma A.1.30 (8)).
Proof of (3): By Lemma 2.3.10, E is finitely generated over the Bernstein
centre ZB, which is a Noetherian local ring. Writing m := rad(ZB), we deduce

In Section A.1.16 we have defined socle and radical filtrations for objects of a
complete and cocomplete abelian category, such as A, C, or Mod(OJGKζ). The next
lemma shows that, for finite length objects, the natural inclusions A →Mod(OJGKζ)
and C →Mod(OJGKζ) preserve these filtrations.
Lemma 2.3.12. If M ∈C has finite length, then the C-socle filtration and the
Mod(OJGKζ)-socle filtration of M coincide. The same statement is true with C
replaced by A, or “socle” replaced by “radical”.
Proof. This is an immediate consequence of Lemma 2.2.31, resp. Lemma 2.2.20.
□

equivalently, the socle filtration of objects of A.

then Lemma A.1.23(2) implies that radn M ⊆ker(q), as desired. This concludes the
proof.
□

2.5. Localization of smooth representations of GL2(Qp). In this and the
next section we recall various results from our paper [DEG23], and explain how
to translate them from the abelian categorical context (which is the focus of that
paper) to the stable ∞-categorical context in which we are working. Along the way,
we establish various relationships between the stable ∞-categories of representations
that we will be considering.
2.5.1. A chain of projective lines. One of the main results of [DEG23] is a localization
theory for A over the Zariski site of a scheme X over F, which is a chain of projective
lines of length (p ± 1)/2, with ordinary double points (where the sign is equal
to −ζ(−1)). There is a bijection between companion pairs of ζ-compatible weights
and the irreducible components3 of X, and we write X(σ|σco) for the component
corresponding to the pair {σ, σco}.
If σ = σa,b is ζ-compatible, and b ̸= p −1, there is a morphism ([DEG23,

We recall the following standard lemma.
Lemma 2.6.3. If A is a coherent ring and M is a finitely generated A-module
for which RHomA(M, –) is of bounded amplitude, then M is compact as an object
of D(A).
Proof. By for example [Wei94, Lem. 4.1.6], M has a finite length resolution by
finitely generated projective A-modules, and is therefore compact in D(A) (see for
example [BN93, Prop. 4.6]).
□
Proposition 2.6.4. The objects of Afp are compact in D(A).

extension groups between parabolic inductions which we will use in the proof of our
main theorem (see Proposition 5.4.21).

• If i = 0 then the rank is 1 if σ1 = σ2, and zero otherwise.

the rank is 2.
• If i = 2 then the rank is 0 if σ1 = σ2 and {σ, σco} is not of type (scalar),
and otherwise the rank is 1.
• If i = 3 then the rank is 0.
3. The moduli stack of rank 2 étale (φ, Γ)-modules
In this section we will recall some of the main results of [EG23; EG22], specialised
to the case of GL2(Qp), and prove some additional results in this setting.

A. DOTTO, M. EMERTON, AND T. GEE

There is a left inverse ψ to φ defined as follows. We have a decomposition A =

Remark 3.1.1. Our notation and conventions differ from that of [EG23], for two
reasons. Firstly, since we are only working over Qp, and not over an extension
K/Qp, we have dropped K from the notation. Secondly, and more significantly, we
use (φ, Γ)-modules for the full cyclotomic extension Qp(ζp∞)/Qp, and we make no
use of (φ, Γ)-modules for the Zp-subextension.
The theories of (φ, Γ)-modules for the two extensions are equivalent (via taking
invariants for the group of roots of unity in Z×
p ), but in most of [EG23] the Zp-
theory was used, because it was convenient when proving the existence of various
moduli stacks to work with a pro-cyclic pro-p group. However, for applications to
the p-adic Langlands program (or more generally in arguments involving explicit
formulas for the actions of φ and Γ), it is much more convenient to work with the
full cyclotomic extension. Since we make no use of the Zp-subextension, and we
want to avoid notational clutter where possible, we have written Γ for the group
denoted eΓ in [EG23], and A, A+ for the rings denoted A′
Qp and (A′)+

in [EG23].

By definition, a projective étale (φ, Γ)-module with A-coefficients is a finitely
generated projective AA-module D, equipped with
• a φ-linear morphism φ : D →D with the property that the corresponding
morphism ΦD : φ∗D →D is an isomorphism (i.e. D is given the structure
of an étale φ-module over AA), and
• a continuous semi-linear action of Γ that commutes with φ.
Here the notion of continuity is with respect to the canonical topology on D inherited
from that on AA (see [EG23, Rem. D.2]). Our étale (φ, Γ)-modules will usually be
assumed to be projective, and we will often write “étale (φ, Γ)-module” for “projective
étale (φ, Γ)-module” when no confusion should arise. We will also sometimes write
“étale (φ, Γ)-module over A” for “étale (φ, Γ)-module with A-coefficients”.
Remark 3.1.2. In the case that A is Artinian, there is the usual equivalence of
categories (see [Fon90]) between the category of (projective) étale (φ, Γ)-module
with A-coefficients and the category of finite projective A-modules with an action
of GQp, given by the functor D 7→(W(C♭) ⊗A D)φ=1.
3.2. Moduli stacks of étale (φ, Γ)-modules.
Definition 3.2.1. We write X for the stack of projective étale (φ, Γ)-modules
of rank 2 with determinant ζε−1, as defined in Definition C.1.1. Explicitly, if A
is a p-adically complete O-algebra, then X(Spf A) is the groupoid of pairs (D, θ)
where D is a rank 2 projective étale (φ, Γ)-module with A-coefficients, and θ is an
identification of ∧2D with (the (φ, Γ)-module corresponding to) ζε−1.

recall in particular that we assume throughout this paper that our Serre weights σ
are compatible with ζ in the sense of Definition 2.1.6.

2

3.3. A continuous map from |X| to |X|. We now construct a continuous map
πss : |X| →|X| from the underlying topological space of the stack X to the underlying
topological space of the chain X of projective lines constructed in Section 2.5.1.
3.3.1. Soberization. Recall that a topological space is sober if every irreducible
closed subset has a unique generic point. The inclusion of the full subcategory of
sober spaces into the category of all topological spaces (and continuous maps) admits
a left adjoint, the soberization of a space, which we will denote sob(–). Concretely
(see e.g. [Stacks, Tag 0A2N]), for any topological space S, we define sob(S) to be the
set of irreducible closed subspaces of S. The topology on sob(S) is defined by letting
its closed subsets be the collection of subsets sob(T), where T ranges over the closed
subsets of S. Of course, the natural morphism (unit of adjunction) S →sob(S) is a
homeomorphism if and only if S itself is sober.
3.3.2. Underlying topological spaces of stacks. If Z is an algebraic stack of finite
presentation over a field k (and so is in particular quasi-compact and quasi-separated),
then we write (as usual) |Z| for the underlying topological space of Z, |Z|ft for its
subset of finite type points and |Z|cl for its subset of closed points. We endow each
of these subsets with the subspace topology. We always have that |Z|cl ⊆|Z|ft,
with equality for schemes (or more generally Deligne–Mumford stacks), but not
in general. We have that |Z|ft is dense in |Z|, and that |Z| is sober [Stacks, Tag
0DQP]. This implies that the inclusion |Z|ft ,→|Z| induces a homeomorphism

for versal rings.

Lemma 3.6.9. Let R be a complete Noetherian local O-algebra with finite residue
field. Let W be a finite type R-module equipped with a continuous (for the mR-adic
topology on W) G-action, admitting a central character, say α : Q×
p →R×. Then α
is a square and W decomposes as a direct sum of eigenspaces Wβ, where β runs
over the various square roots of α in the group of continuous R×-valued characters
of Q×
p , and G acts on Wβ via β ◦det.
Proof. Replacing R by its Artinian quotients, we can assume that R is an Artinian
ring. Then W has the discrete topology, and so the G-action is smooth. Since W has
finite type, the upper unipotent subgroup U has an open subgroup that acts trivially
on W, and conjugating by diag(p−1, 1) we see that U itself also acts trivially. It
follows that wUw−1 acts trivially, hence so does SL2(Qp), which is generated by U
and wUw−1 (see [Col10c, Proposition III.1.1] for a proof). Hence G acts on W via

square root), we can assume that α is trivial. But then the G-action factors through
Q×
p /(Q×
p )2, and the lemma follows.
□

3.7. Defining an open substack of X. In this section, we shift our attention
away from the various stacks Xθ, and back to the stack X. Our main goal is to
describe a certain “good” open substack of X — to be denoted Ugood — and to
describe its underlying reduced substack explicitly.
We begin by noting that since θ has fixed determinant ζω−1, there are only
finitely many θ of types (ssg), (scalar) or (St), even if we allow ourselves to replace F
by Fp. Note furthermore that θ of type (scalar) (resp. of type (St)) exist if and only
if ζ is odd (resp. even).
Definition 3.7.1.
(1) We let Ybad denote the reduced closed substack of X with |Ybad| = π−1

Lemma 3.7.14. If f : T →S is a continuous surjective map of topological spaces,
and T is Noetherian, then the induced map sob(T) →sob(S) is surjective.
Proof. Let A be an irreducible closed subspace of S. Let {Bi} denote the (finite,
because T is Noetherian) set of irreducible components of f −1(A). Then, since f is
surjective, we have A = S f(Bi), and so (since A is irreducible), we have A = f(Bi0)
for one of the components Bi0. Thus the induced map sob(T) →sob(S) is indeed
surjective.
□

4. D♮⊠P1
In this section we study analogues for general coefficients of some of the con-
structions of [Col10c]. Throughout the discussion, all étale (φ, Γ)-modules are
automatically understood to be projective, with the exception that from Section 4.7


## Page 90


90
A. DOTTO, M. EMERTON, AND T. GEE
onward we exploit the relationship between étale (φ, Γ)-modules and Galois repre-
sentations, and so allow ourselves at times to consider étale (φ, Γ)-modules (and
especially so-called formal étale (φ, Γ)-modules) that are not necessarily projective.
Our ring of coefficients is typically taken to be a Noetherian O/ϖa-algebra, for
some a ≥1; the main exception to this occurs in our use of formal étale (φ, Γ)-
modules, where the coefficients are instead taken to be a complete Noetherian local
O-algebra with finite residue field. At certain points we also restrict our coefficients
to be a finite type O/ϖa-algebra; we do this sometimes for topological reasons
(the various topological modules under consideration will then be Polish topological
groups, which lets us apply the open mapping theorem), and sometimes because we
wish to complete at maximal ideals and pass to the formal context.
4.1. Tate modules and topologies. The coefficient rings AA and A+
A introduced
in Section 3.1, and many of the various modules over these rings that we study
below, are examples of Tate A-modules in the sense of [Dri06, §3]. We now recall
this notion and some of its basic properties; see also [EG21, §5] and [EG23, App. D]
for some related results and discussion.
Definition 4.1.1. Let A be a commutative ring. An elementary Tate A-module
is a topological A-module which is isomorphic to P ⊕Q∗, where P, Q are discrete
projective A-modules, and Q∗:= HomA(Q, A) equipped with its natural projective
limit topology (where we write Q∗as the projective limit of the (Q′)∗, where Q′ is
a finite direct summand of Q, and give each (Q′)∗the discrete topology). A Tate
A-module is a direct summand of an elementary Tate A-module.
A morphism of Tate modules is a continuous morphism of the underlying A-
modules.
Definition 4.1.2. A submodule L of a Tate A-module M is a lattice if it is open,
and if furthermore for every open submodule U ⊆L, the quotient L/U is a finitely
generated A-module. A subset of a Tate A-module is bounded if it is contained in
some lattice.
Remark 4.1.3. If A is Noetherian, then a submodule L of a Tate A-module M is
a lattice if and only if it is open and bounded. Indeed if L is open and bounded,
then by definition there is a lattice L′ ⊇L, and for any open submodule U ⊆L, the
quotient L′/U is a finitely generated A-module; so the submodule L/U ⊆L′/U is
also a finitely generated A-module.
If A is an O/ϖa-algebra for some a ≥1, then A+
A and AA are elementary
Tate modules, and A+
A is a lattice in AA; furthermore every finitely generated
projective AA-module has a natural topology, making it a Tate A-module. (See for
example [EG21, Ex. 5.1.13].)
In fact, by [Dri06, Thm. 3.10], there is a natural bijection between finitely
generated projective AA-modules, and pairs (M, T) consisting of a Tate A-module
M and a topologically nilpotent automorphism T : M →M, by giving M the AA-
module structure determined by Tm := T(m). (Here T is topologically nilpotent if
and only if for each pair of lattices L, L′ ⊆M, we have T nL ⊆L′ for all sufficiently
large n.)
Definition 4.1.4. If M is a finitely generated projective AA-module, then we refer
to an A+
A-submodule M ⊂M which is also a lattice as an A+
A-lattice (or A+-lattice
if A is clear from the context).

Remark 4.1.5. Let A be an O/ϖa-algebra. If M is a finitely generated projective
AA-module, and M ⊂M is a lattice, then M need not be an A+
A-lattice. However,
since multiplication by T is topologically nilpotent, for all sufficiently large n we
have T nM ⊂M, so that M is an AJT nK-module. Since M is a finite projective
A((T n))-module, and the rings AJT nK, A((T n)) are respectively isomorphic to A+
A
and AA, this means that we can often reduce questions about lattices to the case of
A+-lattices.
We recall the following lemma due to Drinfeld [EG21, Lem. 5.1.20].
Lemma 4.1.6. If M is a finitely generated projective AA-module, and if M is a
lattice in M, then M/M is A-flat if and only if it is A-projective. If M is furthermore
an A+
A-lattice, then these conditions are in turn equivalent to M being A+
A-projective.
4.1.7. The weak topology on lattices. If A is a complete Noetherian local ring, and
L is a lattice in an A-Tate module, then in addition to its Tate-module topology, it
has a weak topology, as we now explain. Lemma 4.1.8 below shows that we have an
isomorphism (of A-modules, i.e. disregarding topologies) L
∼

←−k L/mk
AL. If we
endow L/mk

L, then the right-hand side inherits an inverse limit topology, which we then can
transport to L via this isomorphism; this is (by definition) the weak topology on L.
Lemma 4.1.8. If A is a complete Noetherian local ring, and L ⊂M is a lattice in
a Tate A-module, then L is mA-adically complete. The A-module L with the weak
topology is an object of Modc(A), hence it is profinite if A has finite residue field.

sum of lattices is a lattice, it suffices to prove the lemma under the assumption that
M = P ⊕Q∗is elementary.

that Q = Q′ ⊕Q′′, we see that Q∗= Q′∗⊕Q′′∗and (Q′)⊥= Q′′∗. Hence there
exists an A-submodule V ⊂Q′∗such that L = V ⊕Q′′∗. Since Q′∗is finite, we see
that V is also finite. By the results of the previous paragraph, this immediately
implies that L is mA-adically complete, and that the weak topology makes L an
object of Modc(A).
Finally, if L ⊂M is any lattice, then it is contained in P ′ ⊕Q∗for some finite
A-summand P ′ ⊂P. Since M = (P/P ′) ⊕(P ′ ⊕Q∗), and (P ′ ⊕Q∗) ∼= (P ′∗⊕Q)∗,
we can conclude the proof by an application of the results in the previous paragraph
(applied with Q∗replaced by (P ′∗⊕Q)∗).
□

Example 4.1.19.
(1) If A is a Noetherian O/ϖa-algebra, then Lemma 4.1.18 applies to finite
projective AA-modules, and hence also to lattices in such modules.

ses of Lemma 4.1.18, then the same is true of the inverse limit M := lim
←−n Mn.

Proposition 4.2.5. Let D be an étale (φ, Γ)-module with A-coefficients.
(1) D contains a maximal A+-lattice D♯⊂D with the property that ψ(D♯) = D♯.
Furthermore, if N is any bounded A+-submodule for which ψ(N) = N, then
N ⊆D♯.
(2) D contains a minimal ψ-stable A+-lattice D♮; furthermore ψ(D♮) = D♮.
(3) We have D♮⊆D♯⊆T −1D♮.
Proof. By Lemma 4.2.4, there exists an A+-lattice M ⊂D with ψ(M) = M.
Let D♯denote the sum of all the A+-lattices M′ ⊂D with ψ(M′) = M′; then
M ⊆D♯⊆T −1M by Lemma 4.2.4, so D♯is an A+-lattice by Lemma 4.1.14 (3).
By construction ψ(D♯) = D♯, and (again by construction) D♯is maximal with this
property. If N is any bounded A+-submodule for which ψ(N) = N, then D♯+ N is
an A+-lattice satisfying ψ(D♯+ N) = D♯+ N. Thus D♯+ N ⊆D♯by the property

Definition 4.3.1. Let D+ be the A-submodule of D consisting of those z ∈D for
which the sequence {φn(z)}n≥0 is bounded. Let D++ be the A-submodule of D
consisting of those z ∈D for which the sequence {φn(z)}n≥0 tends to zero (i.e. the
submodule consisting of those z on which φ acts topologically nilpotently).
Lemma 4.3.2. TD+ ⊆D++ ⊆D+.
Proof. The second inclusion is obvious from the definitions, while the first follows
from the fact that if (an) is a bounded sequence, then (φn(T)an) is a sequence
converging to 0.
□
Lemma 4.3.3. D+ ⊆D♮.
Proof. We follow the proof of [Col10a, Prop. II.5.14]. Fix some z ∈D+. Then
also φ(z) ∈D+, and so Lemma 4.3.2 shows that Tφ(z) ∈D++; i.e. that the
sequence {φn(Tφ(z))}n≥0 tends to zero. Since D♮is a lattice, we thus find that
φn(Tφ(z)) ∈D♮for some sufficiently large value of n. Since D♮is ψ-stable, and
since ψn+1(φn(Tφ(z))) = ψ(Tφ(z)) = ψ(T)z = −z, we thus have z ∈D♮, as
required.
□
The following corollary provides a useful characterization of D+.
Corollary 4.3.4. D+ is an A+
A-lattice in D, and is the (unique) maximal φ-
invariant bounded subset of D.
Proof. We saw in Lemma 4.3.2 that TD+ ⊆D+. Thus, if we show that D+ is a
lattice in D, it is in fact an A+
A-lattice.
By definition any φ-bounded subset of D is contained in D+. In particular, if M
is any φ-stable lattice in D, then M ⊆D+. Such a lattice exists, by [EG21, Lem.
5.2.7], and thus D+ contains a lattice, and so is open. By Remark 4.1.3, this proves
the lemma, provided that we show that D+ is also bounded; but this follows from
Lemma 4.3.3.
□
Our goal in what follows is to show that the formation of each of D++ and
D+ is compatible with flat base change. In the case of D++ this is relatively
straightforward, but the case of D+ is more involved.
A general observation is that, when analyzing constructs such as D+ and D++
(and the related construct Dnr of Definition 4.3.13 below) whose definitions involve
dynamical aspects of the action of φ, complications can sometimes arise from the
existence of a descending chain of ideals in A that do not stabilize. The following
lemma (which is presumably standard) sometimes allows us to get around this
problem, by reducing our analysis to the case of an Artinian coefficient ring, for
which this complication cannot occur.
Lemma 4.3.5. If A is a Noetherian ring, then there exists an embedding A ,→B
with B Artinian.
Proof. First recall that if f is any element of A, then for n large enough, the natural
map A →Af × A/f n is an embedding. Now, if A is non-zero, and if p is a minimal
prime of A, then we may choose an element f ∈A such that the distinguished open
D(f) is a neighbourhood of p in Spec A which contains no other associated primes


## Page 101

101
of A. Thus p is the unique associated prime of Af, and so the natural map Af →Ap
is an embedding. Thus, for n large enough, the natural map A →Ap × A/f n is an
embedding. Since p was chosen to be minimal, the localization Ap is Artinian.
We continue by applying the same argument to A/f n. By Noetherian induction
(in Spec A) the process eventually terminates, and we obtain an embedding of A
into a finite product of Artinian local rings, as required.
□
We now return to the setting of étale (φ, Γ)-modules over Noetherian O/ϖa-
algebras.

Proof. This follows from the fact that each of D+ and D++ is φ-saturated in D
(i.e. if φ(x) is in D+, resp. D++, then so is x itself), as is evident from their
definitions.
□

of the fundamental phenomenon of generically reducible families specializing to
irreducible objects), it should not come as a surprise that Dnr does not behave well
in families; for example, it is not compatible with flat base-change in general.
Lemma 4.3.15. Dnr is a finite type A-submodule of D+.
Proof. Clearly Dnr is φ-stable, while since φ is injective on D we see that Dnr is
also φ-saturated. Since Dnr ⊆φ(D) by definition, we then see that φ(Dnr) = Dnr.
Since D++ is also both φ-stable and φ-saturated, we see that Dnr ∩D++ is again
φ-stable and φ-saturated. It then follows that also φ(Dnr ∩D++) = Dnr ∩D++.
The uniform topological nilpotency of Lemma 4.3.8 then shows that Dnr ∩D++ = 0.

φ(M[T]) ⊇M[T], and hence that φn(M[T]) ⊇M[T] for all n ≥0. Lemma 4.3.11
then implies that M[T] = 0, and so we see that in fact M = 0. Thus Dnr ⊆D+.

to 0.)

hence Dnr is itself a finite type A-module.

The basic motivation for Colmez’s constructions is as follows: we can regard an étale
(φ, Γ)-module D as being the global sections of a P +-equivariant sheaf of A-modules
on Zp. We are then able to extend its P +-equivariance to an equivariance under the
category of all non-degenerate7 piecewise affine-linear maps between open subsets
of Zp, and then (by an appropriate limiting process) to the category of all local
diffeomorphisms (in the sense of Definition 4.4.8 below) between open subsets of Zp.
With these constructions in place, it becomes possible to localize D in an equi-
variant manner over other locally analytic Zp-manifolds, such as P-equivariantly
over Qp, and (our ultimate goal) GL2(Qp)-equivariantly over P1(Qp). In fact, the
exposition becomes simpler if we construct the localization over Qp en route to the
construction of the equivariant structure under local diffeomorphisms, and so we do
this. We discuss localization over P1(Qp) in the next subsection.

4.4.14. Defining – ⊠Qp more generally. For any ψ-stable A-submodule M ⊂D,
we let M ⊠Zp = M, and we let M ⊠Qp denote the set of sequences (xn)n≥0 with

←−ψ M.

subspace topology, and then M ⊠Qp is closed in the infinite product Q
n≥0 M, and

that it is closed, one can for example note that it is the intersection of the sets of
sequences (xn) with ψ(xn+1) = xn for n ≤N, which are closed for any N because ψ

Remark 4.4.15. As Colmez explains in [Col10a, Section V.1.2], Proposition 4.4.10
is motivated by considering the case of A+
A := AJTK with its (φ, Γ)-structure.
Indeed, we may interpret A+

pushforward of measures under f. Here we should note that A+

so in general it is only D that localizes, rather than any of its lattices. Nevertheless,
as we will see in what follows, the lattices D+, D♮, and D♯do interact in important
ways with the localization of D.

(1) The embedding (4.4.17) is open.
(2) We have natural identifications of finite A-modules

4.5. The GL2(Qp)-representations D⊠P1. Continue to let A denote a Noetherian
O/ϖa-algebra, and let D be an étale (φ, Γ)-module with A-coefficients. In the
preceding subsection we have explained how to localize D to a sheaf over Qp,
equivariantly with respect to the action of local diffeomorphisms.
By general
principles, this structure then allows us to localize D over other 1-dimensional

Remark 4.5.8. Since D ⊠pZp = (φ ◦ψ)(D) = φ(D) is a direct summand of
D = φ(D) ⊕Dψ=0, the subspace topology on D ⊠pZp makes it a Tate A-module.
The map φ : D →D ⊠pZp is a topological isomorphism with inverse ψ. Similarly,
D ⊠pnZp is a Tate A-module for all n ∈Z, and it is topologically isomorphic to D
via the action of diag(pZ, 1) ⊆P. The homeomorphism (4.5.6) then shows that
D⊠P1 is a Tate A-module. In particular, it makes sense to talk about A-submodules
of D ⊠P1 being lattices, and we will do so without further comment.
Lemma 4.5.9. Let n ∈Z. Then RespnZp : D ⊠P1 →D ⊠pnZp is open, and sends
lattices to lattices. The same is true for RespnZp : D →D ⊠pnZp if n ≥0.
Proof. By construction, the map RespnZp is continuous, surjective and A-linear. We
now show that it is open. The equivariance properties of RespnZp under the action
of G show that it suffices to prove this when n = 0, in which case ResZp is the
projection onto a direct summand, by Lemma 4.5.5. Hence it is open.
There remains to prove that RespnZp sends lattices to lattices. It suffices to show
that if f : M →N is a surjective, continuous and open morphism between A-Tate
modules, and M ⊂M is a lattice, then f(M) is a lattice in N. Let U ⊂f(M) be
an open A-submodule; we need to prove that f(M)/U is a finite A-module. Since f
induces an isomorphism
M/(M ∩f −1(U)) ∼= f(M)/U,
this is a consequence of the fact that M is a lattice and M ∩f −1(U) is open.
□

Lemma 4.5.11. The action of G on D ⊠P1 that was constructed in Theorem 4.5.1
is continuous.

gx + M, where M ⊂D ⊠P1 is a lattice. Since g ∈G acts continuously on D ⊠P1,
g−1M ⊂D⊠P1 is open. By the continuity of (4.5.12), there is an open subgroup H′
of H and a lattice L ⊂D ⊠P1 such that H′(x + L) ⊆x + g−1M; so (gH′)(x + L) ⊆
gx + M, and gH′ × (x + L) ⊂G × D ⊠P1 is an open neighbourhood of (g, x).

In our later applications, rather than appealing to this continuity directly, we
will use the following consequence of it.
Corollary 4.5.16. The G-action on D ⊠P1 extends to an AJGK-module structure
on D ⊠P1, uniquely characterized by the requirement that the induced AJKK-action
on D ⊠P1 is continuous. Furthermore, the endomorphisms of D ⊠P1 induced by
the elements of AJGK are all continuous.

action of AJKK. Such an extension is unique if it exists (by continuity, together
with the density of A[K] in AJKK), and by the definition of AJGK, this will yield

K · L′ is therefore open and bounded, and so it is a lattice. Replacing K · L′ by

of K-stable lattices Ln. Then each (D ⊠P1)/Ln is a discrete A-module with a
continuous action of K, hence is a smooth K-representation, and thus is canonically
an AJKK-module (with jointly continuous action map). Consequently

Lemma 4.6.6. If M ⊂D ⊠P1 is a B(Qp)-stable lattice, then it is G-stable, and
hence in fact OJGK-stable.
Proof. Since the group G has a neighbourhood basis of the identity given by open
subgroups (e.g. the congruence subgroups of GL2(Zp)), it follows from Lemma 4.5.11
and [EG23, Lem. D.13] that there is an open subgroup H ⊆G such that HM =
M. Since B(Qp)M = M we have B(Qp)HB(Qp)M = M, and since we have
B(Qp)HB(Qp) = G by the Bruhat decomposition, we conclude that M is G-stable.
To see the final assertion, it suffices to note that since O[K] is dense in OJKK,
and since M is closed in D ⊠P1, the K-stability of M implies the OJKK-stability
of M, and G-stability and OJKK-stability together imply OJGK-stability.
□
Proposition 4.6.7. Suppose that there is a B(Qp)-stable lattice M ⊂D ⊠P1.
Then D♮⊠P1 is an OJGK-stable lattice in D ⊠P1.
Proof. By Lemma 4.6.5, we have
D♮⊠P1 ⊆M + ker ResQp ⊆D♯⊠P1.
Since ker ResQp is a finite A-module, by Lemma 4.6.3, it follows that M+ker ResQp
is a lattice. By Lemma 4.5.18, (D♯⊠P1)/(D♮⊠P1) is a finite A-module, hence
D♯⊠P1 is a lattice, and D♮⊠P1 is open in D♯⊠P1, hence D♮⊠P1 is a lattice.
Since D♮⊠P1 is B(Qp)-stable, the proposition follows from Lemma 4.6.6.
□

4.7. Formal (φ, Γ)-modules. In this subsection, which is an interlude in our main
discussion, we briefly extend some of the previous theory to the context of what we
call formal étale (φ, Γ)-modules.
Let R be a complete local Noetherian O-algebra with finite residue field. Following
Dee [Dee01] we let bAR denote the mR-adic completion of AR, and we define a
formal étale (φ, Γ)-module with R-coefficients to be an étale (φ, Γ)-module over
bAR; that is, a finitely generated bAR-module equipped with commuting semilinear
actions of φ and Γ, with the underlying φ-module being étale in the usual sense. We
do not demand that a formal étale (φ, Γ)-module is projective; if it is, we explicitly
refer to it as a projective formal étale (φ, Γ)-module; note that it is then in fact
free, since bAR is a local ring. Note that if R is furthermore a finite O-module, then
bAR = AR, so that the categories of (projective) formal étale (φ, Γ)-modules with
R-coefficients and étale (φ, Γ)-modules with R-coefficients are equivalent.
Of course, in the case that R is Artinian this agrees with the usual definition of
a (not necessarily projective) étale (φ, Γ)-module, and as we recalled in Section 3.1,
in this case there is an equivalence of categories between the category of étale
(φ, Γ)-modules with R-coefficients and the category of finitely generated R-modules
equipped with a continuous action of GQp. We write V 7→D(V ) for the functor
taking a representation of GQp to the corresponding (φ, Γ)-module, and D 7→V(D)
for the quasi-inverse functor.
Returning to the general case that R is a complete local Noetherian O-algebra
with finite residue field, it is shown in [Dee01] that this equivalence extends to
an exact equivalence of categories between the category of formal étale (φ, Γ)-
modules with R-coefficients and the category of finitely generated R-modules with
a continuous action of GQp, where now

This map is invariant under φ and Γ, and by [Col10a, Prop. II.5.19] it descends to a
map
D♯/D♮⊗R ˇDnr →Qp/Zp
which once again is (φ, Γ)-invariant, and puts the two factors in Pontrjagin duality
(which now coincides with Zp-duality, since the two sides have finite Zp-length). By
Lemma 4.7.7 we have
ˇDnr = Dnr((V ∨⊗ε)ab) ∼= Dnr((Vab)∨⊗ε),
so it suffices now to prove that Dnr is compatible with duals and tensor products.
This is standard: for example, in order to prove that the natural map
Dnr(V1) ⊗Zp Dnr(V2) →Dnr(V1 ⊗Zp V2)
is an isomorphism, it suffices to extend scalars to ˘Zp, where it yields the isomorphism
(˘Zp ⊗Zp V1) ⊗˘Zp (˘Zp ⊗Zp V2)
∼
−→˘Zp ⊗Zp (V1 ⊗Zp V2).
□

Lemma 4.7.11. Let R be a complete Noetherian local O-algebra with finite residue
field, and let D be a formal étale (φ, Γ)-module with R-coefficients. Then D♯/D♮
and Dnr are finite R-modules.
Proof. By Lemma 4.7.9 it suffices to prove this for Dnr. Write V := V(D), so that
by Lemma 4.7.7 the lemma is equivalent to the claim that Dnr(V ab) is R-finite.
By definition Dnr(V ab) is mR-adically complete, and since Dnr is exact on abelian
R[GQp]-modules (by Lemma 4.7.7), we know that Dnr(V ab)⊗R F ∼= Dnr(V ab ⊗R F).
By Lemma 4.3.15, the module Dnr(V ab ⊗R F) is F-finite. This implies the required
finiteness by [Stacks, Tag 031D].
□

We also note the following application of the preceding results, which we will
need below. If x ∈Spec(R), we use the notation k(x) to denote the residue field of
the localization of R at the prime ideal corresponding to x.
Lemma 4.7.12. Let R be a reduced O-flat complete Noetherian local O-algebra
with finite residue field.
Let V be a continuous R[GQp]-module which is finite
free over R of rank at least 2, and such that there exists a Zariski dense set of
closed points x ∈Spec(R[1/p]) such that V ⊗R k(x) is an absolutely irreducible
k(x)[GQp]-module. Then Dnr(V ) = 0.
Proof. By Lemma 4.7.7 we need to prove that V ab = 0. Since V ab is O-torsion
free, and V ab[1/p] ⊂V [1/p]ab (where V [1/p]ab is the maximal abelian submodule
of V [1/p]), it suffices to prove that V [1/p]ab = 0. However, V [1/p]ab is contained in
a finite free R[1/p]-module, hence its associated primes are minimal in R[1/p], since
R[1/p] is reduced. To show that V [1/p]ab = 0 it thus suffices to show that it vanishes
after localization at any minimal prime η of R[1/p]. To do so, it suffices to prove that
V [1/p]⊗R k(η) is an absolutely irreducible k(η)[GQp]-module. However, if this is not
the case, then the properness of the Grassmannian of V [1/p] over Spec R[1/p] shows
that V [1/p] ⊗R k(x) is an absolutely reducible k(x)[GQp]-module for all x ∈{η}:
in fact, the GQp-fixed locus is closed in the Grassmannian. This contradicts our
assumption on V .
□
We next establish some properties of the functor D♯⊠Qp. It turns out that this
functor is very well behaved in the formal context, thanks to the following result of
Colmez, which shows that the formation of D♯⊠Qp is exact on étale (φ, Γ)-modules
of finite A-length.
Theorem 4.7.13. Let R be a complete Noetherian local O-algebra with finite residue
field. For any short exact sequence

3 ⊠Qp →0
is also short exact.
Proof. This is immediate from [Col10a, Thm. III.3.5].
□
Corollary 4.7.14. Let R be a complete Noetherian local O-algebra with finite
residue field. Then D 7→D♯⊠Qp is a right exact functor on the category of formal
étale (φ, Γ)-modules with R-coefficients.
Proof. Given an exact sequence 0 →D1 →D →D2 →0 of formal étale (φ, Γ)-
modules with R-coefficients, the sequence (D1)k →Dk →(D2)k →0 is exact for
all k ≥1, and its terms have finite A-length. By Theorem 4.7.13, it stays exact
after applying (–)♯⊠Qp, and then the corollary follows from the exactness of lim
←−k
on profinite R-modules.
□

We now specialize to the case that D has rank 2.
Theorem 4.8.6. Let A be a complete local Noetherian O/ϖa-algebra with finite
residue field, and suppose that DA is an étale (φ, Γ)-module of rank 2 with A-
coefficients and determinant ζε−1.
Then D♮
A ⊠P1 is a OJGK-stable lattice in
DA ⊠P1.
Proof. By Lemma 4.8.5, we may assume (by replacing A by A/mn where m is
the maximal ideal of A) that A is Artinian, in which case DA corresponds to a
2-dimensional representation ρA : GQp →GL2(A). By Lemma 4.8.4 (2), we can
assume that A is a quotient of the universal (fixed determinant) framed deformation
ring S for ρA (mod m).
As explained in [Col10c, §II.3.4], there is a universal formal étale (φ, Γ) module D
over S, and it is proved there that D♮⊠P1 is G-stable; indeed, by [Col10c, Lem. II.3.6],
this follows from the results of Berger–Breuil [BB10] together with the Zariski density
of the so-called crystalline benign points in the generic fibre Spec S[1/p], which
footnote 7 of [Col10c] explains is known for p ≥3. (In fact, Zariski density statements
of this kind are now known in much greater generality; see [BIP23a, Thm. 6.1,
Rem. 6.2] and [BIP23b, Cor. 1.3].) The first paragraph of the proof of [Col10c,
Prop. II.2.15] then shows that D♮
A ⊠P1 is G-stable, and hence also OJGK-stable,
for any Artinian quotient A of S, as required.
□

4.10. Some explicit base-change computations. In this section we investigate

Lemma 4.9.11, the interesting cases are those when D := D(V ), for V a free rank 2
module over the complete Noetherian local ring R equipped with a continuous action
of GQp, such that furthermore V
ss is a twist of ω ⊕1. We will be primarily focused
on the case when V is in fact already semisimple, and so (up to a twist) isomorphic
to ω ⊕1; thus we begin with some considerations related to the corresponding versal
deformation.

5. The functor
We now construct our functor F : Db
fp(A) →Db
coh(X), and prove our main
theorem, i.e. that F is fully faithful.
5.1. The definition of the functor. In this section we construct the pro-coherent
sheaf L∞of OJGKζ-modules over X, and use it to define our functor F.
5.1.1. Interpreting linearly topological modules as pro-coherent sheaves. We begin
by explaining a general procedure that converts certain topological modules into
pro-coherent sheaves. We have the following (slightly ad hoc) definition.
Definition 5.1.2. Let A be a Noetherian ring endowed with the discrete topology.
We say that a linearly topological A-module M is pro-coherent if M is complete, and
if for any open A-submodule U of M, the quotient M/U is finitely generated over A.
(Of course, it suffices to check this condition for U running over a neighbourhood
basis of 0.) Note that the natural morphism M →lim
←−U M/U (the projective limit
being taken over all open submodules of M, where each quotient is endowed with
its discrete topology) is then a topological isomorphism.
Remark 5.1.3. Any lattice in an A-Tate module is pro-coherent when equipped with
its induced topology.
For any finitely generated A-module N, we write e
N to denote the coherent sheaf
obtained by localizing N over Spec A.
Definition 5.1.4. If M is a pro-coherent linearly topological A-module, then we
write f
M := limU ]
M/U, regarded as a pro-coherent sheaf on Spec A, i.e. an object of
Pro Coh(Spec A).
Remark 5.1.5. Any finitely generated A-module may be regarded as a pro-coherent
A-module by endowing it with the discrete topology; in this case the two possible
meanings of e
N evidently coincide.

The functor Modfp(AJKK) →

5.2. Completing L∞. A key tool in our analysis of the functor F will be completion:
both in the sense of completion at a block Aθ of the category A, and completion
along the various substacks Xθ of X. In fact, we will show that these two notions
of completion are compatible, in an appropriate sense, with respect to F (see
Proposition 5.3.6). Relatedly, we will introduce a “completed-at-θ” version of F, and
prove that it is fully faithful (see Theorem 5.2.24). This will be a key ingredient in
the proof that F itself is fully faithful.

We now make some deductions. Firstly, we see that the completion of S (which
we recall is of finite type as a BG-module) at each maximal ideal of BG (which
is itself a finite type F-algebra) is commutative and reduced. Thus S itself is
commutative and reduced, and is in particular a finite commutative BG-algebra.
We have already observed that (5.4.10) makes F[Tp, f(Tp)−1] a finite BG-algebra.
Somewhat confusingly, we have not shown that (5.4.15) is a morphism of BG-
algebras. However, we see from (5.4.18) that (5.4.15) induces isomorphisms of the
completions of its source and target at each maximal ideal nθ of BG. Now the
finiteness of each of F[Tp, f(Tp)−1] and S over BG shows that the maximal ideals of
each of them are partitioned into finite sets according to the maximal nθ of BG that
they lie over, and then the isomorphisms given by the bottom arrow of (5.4.18) show
that (5.4.15) respects these partitions of the maximal ideals in its source and target,
and furthermore induces bijections on each of the finite sets of maximal ideals given
by these partitions. Thus (5.4.15) in fact induces a bijection between the maximal
ideals in its source and target, and an isomorphism between the corresponding
completions of each of its source and target. It follows from Lemma 5.4.19 below
that (5.4.15) is an isomorphism, as desired.
□
Lemma 5.4.19. Let k be a field, and let f : A →B be a morphism between finite
type k-algebras. Assume that f −1 induces a bijection on maximal ideals, and that for
every maximal ideal m ⊂B, the induced map bf : bAf −1(m) →bBm is an isomorphism.
Then f is an isomorphism.
Proof. Since f −1 is bijective on maximal ideals, to prove that f an isomorphism,
it suffices to prove that Spec f is an open immersion. By [Stacks, Tag 02LC], it in
turn suffices to prove that f is étale and universally injective.
For each maximal ideal m of B, it follows from our hypotheses together with [Stacks,
Tag 039M] that the map Af −1(m) →Bm is an étale homomorphism of local rings, and
is in particular flat and unramified. Hence f : A →B is flat, by [Stacks, Tag 00HT]
and furthermore it is unramified at all maximal ideals of B (i.e. (Ω1
B/A)m = 0 for all
maximal m ⊂B), by [Stacks, Tag 039G]. Thus f : A →B is étale.
It remains to show that f is universally injective. By [Stacks, Tag 01S4], it
suffices to prove that B ⊗A B →B induces a surjection on Spec. Since the image of
Spec(B) →Spec(B ⊗A B) is closed, it suffices to show that for each finite extension
k′/k, any morphism ϕ : B ⊗A B →k′ necessarily factors through B. Given such


## Page 171


A CATEGORICAL p-ADIC LANGLANDS CORRESPONDENCE FOR GL2(Qp)
171
a map ϕ, we have two maps ϕ1, ϕ2 : B →k′ (defined by ϕ1(b) = ϕ(b ⊗1) and
